It’s more… I don’t remember the callbacks or work orders I’m supposed to get back to, or little follow-ups I told people I’d handle. Then they pile up, and now it’s enough that work sent me in before it messes up my chance to move up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to remember to do things you planned to do later, like calling someone back or completing a task you promised to finish?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s pretty much the main thing. I’ll tell someone I’ll call back or finish a work order, and then… it just doesn’t happen unless somebody reminds me. It’s piled up enough that work sent me here before it costs me a promotion.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble remembering to do things you planned to do outside of work, like personal errands, calls to friends or family, or other tasks around the house?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the work stuff I’m aware of. The callbacks, work orders, follow-ups I said I’d do… that’s what’s really piled up and why they sent me here. I don’t really have much else to add.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
